### 3. Three Modes of Work

When I say “AI-assisted development,” I don’t mean “open a chat window and vibe-code until something compiles.” That’s how you end up with a grab bag of half-finished refactors and a mysterious failing test you don’t remember creating.

For Token, I forced myself into three very boring-sounding but incredibly useful modes:

- **Build** – add new behavior
- **Improve** – reshape internals without changing observable behavior
- **Sweep** – fix a cluster of related bugs in one pass

Before each session, I’d literally type to the agent: *“This is a **Build** session for split view Phase 3”* or *“This is an **Improve** session to extract modules from `main.rs`—no behavior changes allowed.”* That one line changed everything.

Why? Because the agent’s job is to optimize for whatever you point it at. If you don’t declare the game you’re playing, it will happily mix them:

- “While we’re here, should we also clean up this enum?”
- “This bug would be easier to fix if we rewrote the whole module.”
- “I fixed the test by changing the feature.”

Declaring a mode upfront gives you a contract:

- **Build**: You’re allowed to break things *temporarily* as long as we end up with a new capability.
- **Improve**: You are **not** allowed to change the user-facing behavior; you can only move code around, rename things, and clarify responsibilities.
- **Sweep**: You are allowed to change behavior, but only in service of a very narrow bug cluster.

Concretely, here’s how that played out in Token.

**Build – Split view phases**

The split view feature (multiple editors sharing documents) was a classic Build. The prompt looked like:

> “Implement split view Phase 3: AppModel wiring and layout tree, using EDITOR_UI_REFERENCE.md as the source of truth for viewport geometry.”

The agent wasn’t allowed to “clean up” unrelated modules. It was laser-focused on adding the `EditorArea` layout tree, wiring focus, and respecting the geometry rules in the reference doc.

**Improve – Module extraction sprint**

Later, I had a 3,100-line `main.rs`. That became an **Improve** session:

> “Extract `app.rs`, `input.rs`, `view.rs`, `perf.rs` from `main.rs` without changing behavior. Tests must keep passing.”

The goal was purely structural. At the end, `main.rs` was ~20 lines; the rest of the logic lived in well-named modules. Because the mode was Improve, we could be aggressive about moving code around—but we always had to route back to “same behavior, more understandable shape.”

**Sweep – Multi-cursor selection bugs**

After multi-cursor shipped, I had a bucket of weird selection bugs: overlapping ranges, frozen secondary cursors, inconsistent collapse behavior. Instead of whack-a-mole, I declared:

> “This is a **Sweep** session: fix all known multi-cursor selection issues. Use MULTI_CURSOR_SELECTION_GAPS.md as the checklist.”

The agent then worked through that gap doc: one pass, one area of the system, many small fixes. No new features, no architecture experiments.

The pattern generalizes: whatever you’re building, teach your AI the difference between **Build**, **Improve**, and **Sweep**, and say which one you’re doing before the conversation starts. It’s the cheapest form of scope control you’ll ever add.

---

### 4. Design Before Code

Everyone says “write the docs first.” Almost nobody actually does, because writing docs is slow and not nearly as fun as watching the compiler scream at you.

With AI in the loop, those docs stop being optional. They become the **API** you expose to the agent.

For Token, I ended up with three kinds of design documents that paid off over and over:

1. **Reference docs** – shared truth for tricky domains
2. **Feature specs** – concrete plans for complex features
3. **Gap documents** – “this is 70% done and gross; here’s what’s left”

#### 4.1 Reference Docs: The Geometry Bible

The text editor UI is a geometry problem disguised as a productivity app. You’re constantly converting between:

- Buffer offsets
- Line/column positions
- Pixel coordinates
- Viewport rectangles and scroll offsets

If you try to “just code it,” you get a mess of off-by-one scroll behavior and impossible-to-reason-about helper functions.

So I wrote `EDITOR_UI_REFERENCE.md`: a brutally literal description of how Token’s viewport math works. It defines:

- What a “viewport” is and how it relates to the underlying buffer
- How scrolling behaves for keyboard, mouse wheel, and page navigation
- How we clamp cursors, selections, and scroll positions at document edges
- How split view and overlays share that same geometry

Then I did something slightly masochistic but incredibly useful: I had the Oracle agent review the doc **before** we wrote much rendering code.

That review caught more than 15 issues that would have been bugs in code:

- Inconsistent definitions of “line height” leading to fractional scroll positions
- A division-by-zero edge case when the viewport height was smaller than one line
- Off-by-one rules for “last visible line” vs “line just offscreen”
- Cases where the scroll math would have allowed the cursor to drift slightly offscreen

Fixing those in a markdown file took minutes. Fixing them in code would have required re-threading those assumptions across `view.rs`, `editor_area.rs`, hit testing, and tests.

The hidden benefit: once you have a geometry bible, every future feature (“center cursor on screen,” “overlay search results”) can say “follow the behavior in `EDITOR_UI_REFERENCE.md`” instead of re-litigating what “scroll to reveal” means.

#### 4.2 Feature Specs: Multi-Cursor as a Written Contract

Multi-cursor editing is one of those features where “just implement it” is a trap. There are too many edge cases, and the behavior has to feel **obvious**, not merely “consistent with your current code.”

Before writing serious code, I wrote `SELECTION_MULTICURSOR.md`. The structure was intentionally boring:

- Existing data structures and where they live
- New data structures we’d need
- **Invariants** that must always hold
- Keyboard shortcuts and which messages they fire
- A multi-phase implementation plan
- Success criteria and tests

The invariants section was the moral center of the doc:

```rust
// MUST maintain: cursors.len() == selections.len()
// MUST maintain: cursors[i].to_position() == selections[i].head
```

Those two lines drove a lot of design: it forced us to treat cursors and selections as parallel arrays, updated in lockstep. When we later added deduplication and selection merging, the invariant acted like a unit test written in English.

The message section turned into a clean API surface:

- `EditorMsg::MoveCursorWithSelection(Direction)`
- `EditorMsg::ToggleCursorAtPosition { line, column }`
- `EditorMsg::CollapseToSingleCursor`
- …and so on.

Instead of asking the agent, “How do I support Cmd+Click to add cursors?”, I could say:

> “Implement Phase 4 from `SELECTION_MULTICURSOR.md`: multi-cursor basics. Use the messages and invariants already defined.”

That conversation is dramatically easier to keep on track. The agent isn’t inventing behavior on the fly; it’s filling in code against a spec we already agreed on.

#### 4.3 Gap Documents: From “Feels Buggy” to Checklists

The third document type only appears later in a feature’s life: when something is 60–90% done, working *mostly* fine, but clearly not “finished.”

For multi-cursor, that was `MULTI_CURSOR_SELECTION_GAPS.md`. The pattern:

1. **What’s implemented**
2. **What’s missing or broken**
3. **Design decisions for each gap**
4. **Tests / success criteria**

For example, instead of “selection merging seems off,” the gap doc would say:

- Implemented:
  - `merge_overlapping_selections()` merges overlapping or touching ranges.
  - Parallel vectors `cursors` and `selections` are updated in lockstep.
- Missing:
  - Tests for adjacent selections like `[0,5)` and `[5,10)`.
  - Behavior when three or more selections collapse to the same position.
- Design notes:
  - Adjacent selections should merge into a single range.
  - When merged, keep the lowest index as the “primary” cursor.
- Tests:
  - Given cursors at positions A, B, C with overlapping selections, assert we end up with one cursor/selection pair satisfying the invariants.

Gap docs are where the “AI loves checklists” thing really shows. Models struggle with “this feels buggy.” They excel at:

> “Here is a list of 9 concrete gaps. For each, either implement the missing behavior or add an explicit TODO explaining why we’re deferring it. Keep the invariants intact.”

Because the doc already encodes context, each Sweep session becomes:

> “Work through `MULTI_CURSOR_SELECTION_GAPS.md` top to bottom. Do not add new shortcuts or data structures.”

That’s the pattern I’ve come back to repeatedly:

- Reference docs for **shared physics of the system**
- Feature specs for **big changes**
- Gap docs for **polish and bug sweeps**

None of this is glamorous, but it turns vague frustration into structured work the agent can actually help with.

---

### 5. Agent Configuration

The last piece of the framework is the least exciting and the most leveraged: **tell the agent how your project works. Explicitly. In one place.**

For Token, that’s `AGENTS.md` (and a mirrored `CLAUDE.md`):

- How to build and run the project
  - `make dev`, `make test`, `make test-one TEST=...`
- Where the important modules live
  - “Editor state is in `src/model/`, rendering in `view.rs`, perf overlays in `perf.rs`”
- Naming conventions
  - How we name messages, models, and modules
- Any “gotchas”
  - “Don’t call `cargo test` directly; always use `make test` so the right feature flags and environment variables are set.”

Before this file existed, the AI did what any well-meaning junior dev would do: it made stuff up.

It would propose commands like:

- `cargo test --all-features --no-fail-fast`
- `cargo run --bin token-editor`

Technically reasonable, completely wrong for this project.

Once `AGENTS.md` spelled out:

```text
To run tests: use `make test` or `make test-one TEST=...`.
To run the app: use `make dev` during development.
Do NOT invent cargo flags; rely on the Makefile.
```

…the hallucinated incantations basically stopped. Every “run tests” instruction in a prompt could now say, “and verify with `make test`,” and the agent followed suit.

You don’t need an elaborate setup to copy this pattern. Even for a small project, a single page is enough:

- **How to build and run**
- **How to run tests**
- **Where the core logic lives**
- **Any non-obvious constraints** (“this script must run from the repo root,” “this service requires Redis running locally,” etc.)

Then you reference that page in your prompts:

> “You’re working in the `Improve` mode. Before proposing changes, skim `AGENTS.md` to understand how to run tests and where the editor state lives.”

It’s boring glue. But it’s the difference between an AI that constantly trips over your build system and one that feels like a teammate who read the onboarding doc before touching anything.

### 6. Case Study #1 – Multi‑Cursor Migration

The single biggest change in Token was going from “there is one cursor” to “there might be N cursors at arbitrary positions.” That sounds like a cute feature until you realize “single cursor” was baked into almost every file: movement, selection, delete/insert, viewport math, tests, even helper names.

A full rewrite would have been a great way to ship nothing for three weeks, so we treated it as a structured migration instead of a heroic refactor.

#### Start with invariants, not code

Before touching any code, we wrote down what *must* stay true:

```rust
// MUST maintain: cursors.len() == selections.len()
// MUST maintain: cursors[i].to_position() == selections[i].head
```

These invariants showed up everywhere: in the design doc, in comments, and eventually in tests. They became the “contract” we could repeatedly hand to AI agents: “you can change internals, but if these conditions ever break, you’ve done something wrong.”

#### Migration helpers: pretending we still had one cursor

The next trick was to *lie* to the rest of the codebase.

Internally, `EditorState` grew a `Vec<Cursor>`, but we kept an old‑looking accessor so existing handlers and tests didn’t explode immediately:

```rust
// Migration helper - old code still works
impl AppModel {
    pub fn cursor(&self) -> &Cursor { &self.editor.cursors[0] }
}
```

Anywhere that wasn’t ready to think “multi‑cursor” could keep using `cursor()` and stay blissfully ignorant. This bought us the freedom to convert one subsystem at a time instead of “convert everything or nothing compiles.”

#### Phased implementation: from primitives to behavior

We then split the migration into numbered phases that an AI agent could execute independently:

- **Phase 0 – Per‑cursor primitives.**
  Add low‑level helpers that operate on “cursor at index i” without worrying about “all cursors” yet:

  ```rust
  // Per-cursor primitive + all-cursors wrapper
  fn move_cursor_left_at(&mut self, doc: &Document, idx: usize) { ... }
  fn move_all_cursors_left(&mut self, doc: &Document) {
      for idx in 0..self.cursors.len() {
          self.move_cursor_left_at(doc, idx);
      }
  }
  ```

- **Phase 1 – All‑cursors wrappers.**
  Once the per‑cursor operations existed, we layered “for all cursors” functions on top (`move_all_cursors_left`, `move_all_cursors_right`, etc.), always ending with a dedup step when cursors collided.

- **Phase 2–3 – Wire up handlers.**
  Movement handlers in `update` (arrows, Home/End, word movement, PageUp/Down) were switched from `cursor_mut()` to the new `move_all_*` APIs. Selection variants got separate “with_selection” helpers so we didn’t accidentally collapse selections.

- **Phase 4 – Tests.**
  We added explicit multi‑cursor tests (“two cursors at positions X and Y both move left”, “colliding cursors are deduped”, “each cursor keeps its own desired_column”) while keeping all single‑cursor tests green.

- **Phase 5 – Bug sweep.**
  Once behavior was mostly right, we ran a dedicated Sweep session: grep for everything that still looked suspiciously single‑cursor and either delete the helper (`cursor_mut`) or wrap it in a multi‑cursor equivalent.

This “0‑5” structure mapped perfectly to the Build/Improve/Sweep modes from earlier: Build the new APIs, Improve the architecture around them, then Sweep for edge cases.

#### What went wrong anyway

Despite the planning, we still stepped on a few rakes.

**1. Some handlers quietly stayed single‑cursor.**
A few movement paths were still calling helpers like `first_non_whitespace_column()` that implicitly read from the primary cursor’s line. Everything compiled, most tests passed, and yet: only the main cursor jumped to the right column while the others stayed put. The fix was to push those helpers down into the per‑cursor primitives so every cursor computed its own target column.

**2. “Only primary cursor moved” – again.**
Even after introducing `move_all_cursors_left`, some call sites still used the old `move_cursor_left` out of habit. The symptom was familiar: you’d add multiple cursors, hit an arrow key, and only index 0 moved. The Sweep mode here was basically: list all the places that can move the cursor, then systematically assert “this uses an all‑cursor API, not a single‑cursor one.”

**3. Overlapping selections and the late arrival of `merge_overlapping_selections()`.**
The really fun bug showed up in real usage: add cursors on adjacent words, run “Select Word” a few times, and suddenly you’d get overlapping or touching selections that broke rendering and copy/paste semantics.

The data structure invariants were still technically true—`cursors.len() == selections.len()`—but the *user expectations* invariant (“overlapping selections should behave like one big selection”) was not. The fix was to add:

```rust
pub fn merge_overlapping_selections(&mut self) {
    // 1. Sort by start position
    // 2. Merge consecutive overlapping/touching ranges
    // 3. Update both cursors and selections arrays in lockstep
}
```

That function didn’t exist in the original plan; it emerged from actually *using* multi‑cursor selection for a while and then formalizing “what feels right” into another invariant.

The takeaway: the framework helped us avoid total chaos, but it didn’t make us omniscient. We still missed cases—what changed is that we had a clear place to record them (gap docs) and a repeatable way to sweep them up with the AI’s help.

---

### 7. Case Study #2 – Split View Architecture

Split view was the other “touch everything” feature: multiple panes, tabs, and shared documents, all without turning the codebase into a tangle of special cases.

The naïve approach would have been to thread `Option<SecondaryEditor>` through half the code and pray. Instead, we treated it as an architectural upgrade with aggressive use of migration helpers so old code could keep thinking “there is one document, one editor” while the new world came online behind the scenes.

#### The core architecture

We made three key decisions:

1. **Documents are shared, editors are not.**
   Documents live in a `HashMap<DocumentId, Document>`. Editors live in a separate `HashMap<EditorId, EditorState>`. Any number of editors can point at the same document, each with its own cursors, selections, and viewport. This matches how real editors behave: split the view, but don’t duplicate the file.

2. **Layout is a tree, not a grid of booleans.**
   We introduced a `LayoutNode` tree to represent editor groups and splits. A node is either a `Group` (a tab strip with one active editor) or a `Split` that contains children with a split direction and size ratios. That gives you arbitrary nesting: two vertical splits on the left, one big editor on the right, etc.

3. **`EditorArea` owns the mess.**
   Instead of sprinkling layout logic through input, rendering, and state, we concentrated it in an `EditorArea` type that knows:
   - which editor is focused,
   - which document each editor shows,
   - how the layout tree maps to actual rectangles on screen.

#### Migration helper: `single_document()` as a compatibility layer

The problem, of course, is that the rest of the codebase still assumed there was a single `Document` and a single `EditorState`. We didn’t fix that overnight.

Instead, we created migration helpers that *pretended* the new architecture was still the old one. Conceptually:

```rust
impl AppModel {
    pub fn document(&self) -> &Document {
        self.editor_area.focused_document()  // Hides complexity
    }
}
```

Everywhere that previously grabbed `model.document` or `model.editor` could keep doing so. Internally, those accessors simply delegated to “whatever editor is currently focused in the layout tree.”

This gave us two huge advantages:

- We could land the `HashMap<DocumentId, Document>` / `HashMap<EditorId, EditorState>` / `LayoutNode` refactor *first*, behind stable accessors.
- Then we could gradually introduce split‑specific behavior (open in new group, close split, cycle focus) without breaking the rest of the app every time.

#### AI in phases, not as an all‑knowing architect

Split view was implemented almost entirely through AI sessions, but never as “please build split view.” Instead, we split it into narrow phases:

- Phase 1: Define data structures (`DocumentId`, `EditorId`, `LayoutNode`, `SplitContainer`).
- Phase 2: Implement layout traversal and basic hit‑testing.
- Phase 3: Wire rendering to the layout tree (each group renders its active editor).
- Phase 4: Add commands to create/destroy splits and move focus.
- Phase 5: Sweep for all the places that assumed `AppModel` had a single document/editor.

Each phase came with a small spec (“what’s the shape of the tree,” “what does ‘focused group’ mean,” etc.), and the migration helpers acted as guardrails. If something broke, it broke *inside* `EditorArea`, not everywhere.

The end result looks like a clean, intentional architecture. In reality, it was a chain of small, reversible steps, plus a lot of lying to the old code until it was ready to face the truth.

---

### 8. Case Study #3 – The Cmd+Z Disaster

Not every story is a grand architecture migration. Sometimes you just press Cmd+Z and your editor confidently types the letter `z` into the document.

That actually happened.

On macOS, the Command key is reported as a different modifier than Control. Token’s undo handler, however, was enthusiastically Windows‑pilled: it only checked `control_key()`. So on macOS, “Cmd+Z” looked exactly like “plain Z” and fell through to the text input path.

Here’s the embarrassing version:

```rust
// Before (broken on macOS)
if modifiers.control_key() && key == "z" { ... }
```

If you’re on Linux or Windows, you never notice. On macOS, your first undo is a literal `z` character. Not ideal.

#### From single bug to Sweep mode

We could have fixed just that line and called it a day. Instead, we asked the more uncomfortable question: “If undo is wrong, what else is wrong?”

The answer, predictably, was “quite a bit.” Several other shortcuts were also only checking `control_key()` and ignoring `super_key()`. Some were subtly inconsistent across platforms. Rather than whack moles one at a time, we flipped into Sweep mode:

- **Define the rule:** For cross‑platform shortcuts, treat “Ctrl on Windows/Linux” and “Cmd on macOS” as equivalent. That means always checking both `control_key()` and `super_key()` for editor commands.
- **Search the codebase:** Grep for every place we looked at modifiers + keys.
- **Normalize behavior:** Update each shortcut to follow the same pattern, add comments where necessary, and make tests (or at least manual test notes) reflect the intended cross‑platform mapping.

The final form of the undo check became:

```rust
// After (cross-platform)
if (modifiers.control_key() || modifiers.super_key()) && key == "z" { ... }
```

Not exactly rocket science—but only once you’ve remembered that `super_key()` exists.

#### Why this matters for working with AI

This was a tiny change in terms of code, but a good example of how to use AI productively:

- In **Build** mode, we had originally implemented undo/redo quickly, focusing on getting behavior right on one platform.
- Once the bug surfaced, we switched to **Sweep** mode: “audit all keyboard shortcuts for cross‑platform correctness,” not just “fix Cmd+Z.”
- The AI could then take the simple rule (“Ctrl or Cmd should both trigger these commands”) and apply it everywhere, instead of us manually eyeballing 20 handlers and inevitably missing one.

It also reinforced a more humbling lesson: no amount of upfront design will stop you from occasionally shipping “Cmd+Z inserts z” into your own editor. But if you’ve set up clear modes of work and you’re willing to turn an embarrassing bug into a structured sweep, you at least get a better system out of it—and a good story for a blog post.
```

### 9. What AI Was Good At (And Where It Faceplanted)

By the end of this project, the pattern was pretty clear: AI is fantastic at certain shapes of work, and absolutely terrible at others.

On the plus side, it crushed anything that looked like “apply well-specified rules at scale.”

- **Turning docs into code.**
  Once I sat down and wrote `EDITOR_UI_REFERENCE.md`—with concrete cursor/scroll rules, coordinate systems, and examples—the assistant suddenly became a machine for producing consistent view/scroll math.
  I’d say: “Update all the scrolling functions to use the reference doc’s coordinate conventions” and it would methodically fix each one, stay consistent across files, and even point out spots where my older code violated my own rules. Before that doc existed, similar prompts gave me a new, slightly different convention every time.

- **Phased refactors.**
  Extracting `main.rs` into modules should have been stressful. Instead, I broke it into phases across seven threads: first isolate config loading, then input handling, then rendering, etc. Each session I’d say something like: “We’re in Improve mode; goal is to extract input handling into `input.rs`, no behavioral changes, tests must pass.”
  The AI happily did the grunt work: moving functions, fixing imports, cleaning signatures. I mostly reviewed diffs and nudged naming. We never lost test coverage, and each phase stayed bite-sized.

- **Systematic sweeps.**
  When you tell it “find every handler that touches cursors and normalize how we update selections,” it shines. Same with “update every shortcut that uses modifiers to support Mac and Linux keymaps.”
  This is exactly the kind of repetitive, easy-to-miss work humans get sloppy with at 11 p.m. and AI just…doesn’t get tired.

- **Research → synthesis.**
  The Librarian + Oracle pattern was a big win. I’d have the “Librarian” agent go read Zed’s GPUI docs, GitHub issues, and blog posts, then ask the higher-level “Oracle” agent: “Given this research, what’s a sane strategy for mapping my keymap and modifier behavior?”
  Instead of me skimming 10 tabs, it gave me a compact summary and trade-offs. I still made the final call, but it lined up the options and explained them in my language.

Where it **faceplanted** was wherever I tried to outsource judgment, taste, or long-term memory.

- **Inventing architecture from nothing.**
  Early on, I asked some version of: “What should the editor model look like? Design the whole thing.”
  The answers looked impressive—nice boxes and arrows, plausible module names—but they were full of hand-wavy bits: “we’ll have a `SelectionManager` that efficiently handles all selection cases” without acknowledging the gnarly invariants or performance constraints I cared about.
  The moment I pushed on details (“how exactly do we keep multi-cursor selections consistent during reflow?”), the design crumbled or flipped to a different abstraction. It was a great brainstorming partner, but a terrible chief architect.

- **Remembering subtle invariants across days.**
  Anything that lived only in my head would get violated 2–3 sessions later. For example, I had an unwritten rule that “a selection’s end should never be before its start; we normalize on every update.”
  A few days after establishing that rule in passing, we did a multi-cursor delete refactor. The AI “simplified” some code paths and suddenly we had inverted selections showing up in edge cases. That was on me: I’d never written the invariant down. Once it lived in `EDITOR_UI_REFERENCE.md`, the AI started defending it instead of breaking it.

- **Vague tasks.**
  The worst prompts were things like: “Make selection feel better.” What came back was a mix of random tweaks: changed thresholds here, adjusted scroll speed there, touched three different modules. It was unreviewable and untestable.
  The same problem, framed as: “Fix `DeleteBackward` for multi-cursor newline adjustment; current bug is X, expected behavior is Y” turned into a clean, surgical patch with a test.

**Failure anecdote #1:**
I once asked it to “rethink the whole selection architecture for performance and clarity.” It dutifully generated a multi-layered design with managers and services, then started a huge refactor touching dozens of files. Halfway through, tests exploded and the new abstractions didn’t actually solve the bottleneck. I ended up discarding the branch and instead wrote a tiny “Selection invariants + hot paths” doc and asked it to optimize just those hot paths. That worked.

**Failure anecdote #2:**
Another time, after a good day working on cursor behavior, I came back two days later and asked, “Extend the previous selection logic to support block selections.” I assumed the AI “remembered” the subtleties we’d just ironed out. It didn’t. It reintroduced an off-by-one bug we’d already fixed. The fix was not new code—it was me copying the invariant from the last session into the reference doc and telling it: “These rules are non-negotiable; apply them here too.”

---

### 10. Framework Recap – Apply This to Your Project

If you want to steal this approach wholesale, here’s the distilled version. You don’t need an editor project; this works for any non-trivial codebase.

1. **Pick a non-trivial, bounded project.**
   Don’t start with “rebuild our entire monolith” or “redesign our infra.” Pick something that’s big enough to be real but small enough to finish: a new feature flag system, a refactor of your auth flows, a draft of a new syncing layer.

2. **Write a 1–2 page reference doc for your core domain.**
   This is the keystone. Define your vocabulary, invariants, coordinate systems, failure modes. For an editor it was cursor semantics and scrolling rules. For you it might be “how sessions, tokens, and users relate” or “what ‘order status’ actually means at each step.”
   Don’t aim for completeness—aim for “good enough that a new teammate (or AI) would make the same choice you would on 80% of questions.”

3. **Define mode at each session start.**
   At the top of the thread, declare:
   - Mode: **Build** (new capability), **Improve** (refactor/optimize), or **Sweep** (systematic, repetitive changes).
   - Goal: one clear outcome.
   - Non-goals: a few things you explicitly won’t touch.
   This single habit reduced scope creep more than anything else. It also made it easier to say “not now” when the AI suggested interesting but off-track changes.

4. **Create a simple `AGENTS.md`.**
   One small doc that answers:
   - How to run tests
   - How to run the app
   - Where the key docs live
   - Any “house rules” (coding style, commit conventions, etc.)
   Think of it as the README for your AI pair. Every new thread starts with: “Context is in `AGENTS.md`; we’re in Improve mode, see goal below.”

5. **For big features: write a feature spec + phased plan.**
   Before touching code, outline:
   - What success looks like (user-visible behavior, constraints)
   - A 2–4 phase plan (e.g., data model, API, UI, sweep)
   Then execute one phase per thread. “Phase 1 only; no UI tweaks yet.” The AI will happily try to ship everything in one go unless you gate it like this.

6. **When you’re “almost done but something’s weird,” write a gap doc.**
   Instead of asking, “Why does this feel janky?” pause and jot a short “Gap Notes” doc:
   - What works
   - What feels off, with concrete examples
   - Any hypotheses
   Then give *that* to the AI: “We’re in Improve mode; address these gaps, don’t restructure anything else.” This keeps you from random, unprincipled tweaking.

7. **Schedule bug sweeps instead of random drive-bys.**
   Rather than fixing bugs wherever you spot them, collect them. Then run a dedicated “Sweep” session: “Fix all cursor blinking edge cases from BUGS.md; no refactors.”
   The AI is great at “find every instance of X and patch it.” Use that. It’s terrible at “oh, while we’re here let’s secretly refactor three modules.”

That’s it. If you do nothing else:

- Write one good reference doc.
- Declare a mode and goal at the start of each session.
- Keep work in small, phased chunks.

Everything else is just scaffolding to support those three habits.

---

### 11. Failure Modes and Anti-Patterns

I also learned a few ways to use AI that absolutely do not work. Ask me how I know.

- **Chatbot-driven development.**
  This is when you open a chat and say, “Just build the feature” with no docs, no tests, and a fuzzy description. You might get code that compiles, but under the surface it’s inconsistent with the rest of your system, ignores edge cases, and silently invents behaviors you’ll be stuck with.
  If you see the AI guessing at your domain instead of quoting your docs back to you, that’s a smell.

- **Treating each session as a fresh start.**
  Early on, every day was a new chat with a different mini-spec. No shared `AGENTS.md`, no stable reference docs. The result was a pile of slightly incompatible decisions. I’d fix something Tuesday and unknowingly undo it Friday.
  Once I forced everything through the same small set of living docs, the AI stopped contradicting itself.

- **Letting AI reorganize the codebase “for cleanliness.”**
  One day I let it go wild: big refactor, new modules, “clearer” layering, all in one Improve-mode session that wasn’t actually scoped. It renamed files, moved types, adjusted imports, and touched dozens of call sites.
  Halfway through reviewing the diff, I realized I didn’t actually know what problem we were solving. Tests were green, but I’d lost my mental map. That was the moment I stopped the experiment, created a `ROADMAP.md` and a `CHANGELOG.md`, and wrote down:
  - What we’re changing this week
  - Why we’re changing it
  - What landed each day
  From then on, no large reorgs without a crisp Improve-mode goal tied directly to the roadmap.

- **Mixing Build, Improve, and Sweep in one thread.**
  “Let’s add a new selection mode, clean up the old code, and also fix some related bugs” sounds efficient. In practice, it’s a mess. You get changes that are impossible to review because every file has new behavior, refactors, and bugfixes all tangled together.
  When I forced myself to pick one mode per thread, the quality of AI-produced diffs jumped immediately.

The pattern behind all of these: AI amplifies whatever process you give it. If your process is vague, impulsive, and undocumented, you’ll get that back at scale. If you constrain it with clear docs, modes, and small phases, it becomes an extremely fast, very patient teammate instead of a chaos engine.
```

1) TL;DR: Here are polished drafts for Sections 12–13: a concrete timeline that makes the work feel real and deliberate, and a grounded closing that re-emphasizes process over “prompt magic” and invites the reader to try it.

---

### 12. Timeline – How It Actually Unfolded

This wasn’t a weekend toy project. It moved in deliberate phases, each with a narrow objective and a clear definition of “done,” so we didn’t get lost in the usual “what if we also…” spiral.

| Phase               | Dates          | Focus                                                  |
|---------------------|----------------|--------------------------------------------------------|
| Foundation          | Sep 26 - Dec 5 | Setup, reference docs, architecture                    |
| Research Sprint     | Dec 6          | Performance, keymaps, testing infrastructure           |
| Feature Development | Dec 5-6        | Split view, undo/redo, multi-cursor selection         |
| Codebase Refactor   | Dec 6          | Extract modules from main.rs (3100→20 lines)          |
| Research & Polish   | Dec 7          | Zed research, cursor API fixes, test extraction       |
| Maintenance         | Dec 7          | Bugfixes, benchmarks, documentation                   |

Foundation was about getting the scaffolding right: Makefiles, docs, invariants. The research sprint narrowed in on performance and ergonomics before we piled on features. Feature development was intentionally constrained: three capabilities, then stop. The refactor phase existed specifically to keep main.rs from becoming a junk drawer. Research & polish closed gaps that surfaced under real use, and maintenance was about answering the boring questions—benchmarks, docs, and bugfixes—so this could survive contact with reality.

Each phase had a job. When that job was done, we moved on.

---

### 13. Closing

AI didn’t magically build a text editor. It amplified a very old-school process: write reference docs, define invariants, move in phases, and refuse to skip the unglamorous steps.

The “magic” wasn’t in clever prompts. It was in having something solid to point the model at: reference docs that explained intent, gap docs that made missing pieces explicit, Makefiles that encoded the workflow, and tests that enforced invariants. The model just made it cheaper to iterate on that backbone.

If you want to try this yourself, don’t start with a greenfield editor. Pick one gnarly subsystem you already own—parsing, syncing, rendering, whatever—and give it the same treatment: a reference doc, a gap doc, a tiny phase plan, and a test harness. Use AI as the fast pair-programmer living inside that structure.

If it goes wrong, that’s not a failure of “AI” in the abstract; it’s one more failure mode you’ve discovered for next year’s post.

For the full play-by-play (including dead ends and reversals), here’s the complete thread reference (58 threads) if you want to see every step up close.

  "content": "## Text Editors Are a Lie\n\n\"It's just characters and a blinking cursor, right?\"\n\nThat's what I thought too, before I tried to actually build one.\n\nOn the surface, a text editor is a rectangle with some glyphs and a caret. Underneath, it's a tiny physics engine for text, juggling a dozen rules you never consciously think about.\n\n**Cursor and selection choreography:** hold Shift and tap the arrow keys. The cursor moves one way, the selection edge moves another, sometimes the viewport scrolls a little, sometimes it doesn't. Double-click a word, then Shift+Click ten lines down. Obviously the selection should be everything between those points… except there are about twelve \"obvious\" interpretations once you start writing tests.\n\n**Keyboard modifier edge cases:** what does Ctrl+Left mean on a line that mixes emoji, snake_case, and camelCase? When you hit Cmd+Z twice, then type a character, what exactly happens to the selection history? On macOS, is that Ctrl+Z or Cmd+Z or both? (Ask me how I ended up with an editor where Cmd+Z just typed the letter \"z\" into the file.)\n\n**Grapheme clusters:** that \"one character\" on screen might be multiple Unicode codepoints fused together—accents, zero-width joiners, ligatures. The cursor should never park in the middle of \"half a glyph,\" but your byte offsets, visual columns, and rendered width all disagree about what \"position 5\" even means.\n\n**Viewport scrolling:** Page Down is not just `scroll_y += viewport_height`. It cares which line becomes the new top, how much of the previous context to keep, and where the cursor lives inside a fuzzy \"safe zone\" so you don't get seasick while typing.\n\nAll of that lives behind a UI that's supposed to feel invisible.\n\nNow imagine asking an AI: \"Hey, can you help me implement a text editor? It's just characters and a blinking cursor.\"\n\nWhat follows is either magic or a train wreck, depending entirely on how you work with it.\n\nThis post is about the version that (mostly) worked.\n"

  "content": "## Why I Tried This With AI\n\nIf I wanted to demonstrate AI-assisted development in a sane way, I probably should have built a REST API, a to-do app, or yet another \"AI email summarizer.\"\n\nInstead, I decided: you know what would be fun? Let's write a desktop text editor in Rust. With multi-cursor support. And JetBrains-ish vibes. And let's have AI help with basically all of it.\n\nThe project is called **Token**: a multi-cursor code editor, written in Rust, inspired by JetBrains IDEs. It has split views, an overengineered scrolling model, and far too many opinions about cursor behavior. As of writing, it's the result of **58+ AI threads** and **hundreds of tests** (400+ at one point, eventually 600+ as things escalated).\n\nWhy a text editor? Because it's the perfect \"this should be simple / oh god it's not\" project. It looks trivial from the outside, but the internals are just complex enough that:\n\n- You can't brute-force it in one giant prompt.\n- Hand-waving \"eh, the cursor moves\" stops working after the third bug.\n- Architectural mistakes hurt, but not so much that you give up.\n\nIn other words: exactly the kind of project where working *with* AI will either be exposed as theater… or prove actually useful.\n\nAnd I didn't want to write **another course**. There's no \"guru voice\" here, no secret prompt formula, no email gate. I'm not going to tell you to \"just think in systems\" and \"leverage the power of agents\" as if that means anything.\n\nWhat I *am* going to do is walk through the patterns that actually helped me ship a real, non-trivial thing *with* AI:\n\n- How I structured work so the models didn't drown in context.\n- How I turned \"text editor vibes\" into concrete invariants and documents.\n- How I stopped asking \"build the whole feature\" and started asking \"fix this specific gap.\"\n\nSome of this went surprisingly well. Some of it is embarrassing in hindsight (like the week I spent fighting a scrolling bug that turned out to be an off-by-one I'd literally documented in my own spec).\n\nBut if you're the kind of person who wants to build real software with AI—not just screenshot cool chat logs—then those scars are probably more useful than a polished curriculum.\n"
}
```

{
  "path": "~/code/website/src/pages/articles/ai-assisted-development-real-projects/sections/02-core-principle.md",
  "content": "## The Core Principle\n\nHere's the one sentence that, in hindsight, explains 90% of what worked:\n\n**AI agents excel at focused, well-defined tasks. The human's job is to provide structure.**\n\nWhen I treated the AI as \"the senior engineer who will just figure it out,\" I got garbage: sprawling diffs, half-baked abstractions, and that weird uncanny-valley code that technically compiles but clearly doesn't belong.\n\nWhen I treated it like a very fast, very literal junior dev—with clear specs, boundaries, and checklists—it became genuinely useful.\n\nMy job wasn't to be a \"prompter.\" My job was to be the **project manager and architect**:\n\n- I decided the phases: \"Today we're doing selection behavior,\" not \"let's just improve the editor.\"\n- I wrote the boring documents: what a viewport is, how scroll offsets work, what \"keep cursor visible\" really means.\n- I defined invariants: `cursors.len() == selections.len()` is not a suggestion; it's a law.\n\nAny time I got lazy and skipped that structure, I paid for it.\n\nAt one point, I opened a thread with something like: \"The editor feels janky when I scroll and move the cursor. Can you clean up the scrolling logic?\" That was it. No spec, no constraints, no examples.\n\nThe AI did exactly what I asked: it \"cleaned things up.\" It merged functions, renamed variables, rearranged conditionals. It also silently broke the subtle \"safe zone\" around the cursor, introduced a new off-by-one on Page Down, and made selection jumps worse in certain cases. The code *looked* nicer. The behavior was a mess.\n\nContrast that with later sessions, where I came in with:\n\n- A tiny, explicit problem: \"When the cursor moves below the viewport, we jump too far. Here's the current scroll algorithm and three failing scenarios.\"\n- A written spec snippet: \"The cursor should stay within N lines of the top/bottom; here are examples.\"\n- A clear success condition: \"Fix these tests, don't change semantics anywhere else.\"\n\nSame AI, same codebase, radically different outcome.\n\nSo that's the frame for the rest of this post: not \"how to prompt harder,\" but how to turn your project into a series of **well-defined tasks** that a machine can actually help with—while you stay in charge of the structure.\n"
}
```

{
  "path": "~/code/website/src/pages/articles/ai-assisted-development-real-projects/sections/03-three-modes.md",
  "content": "## Three Modes of Work\n\nWhen I say \"AI-assisted development,\" I don't mean \"open a chat window and vibe-code until something compiles.\" That's how you end up with a grab bag of half-finished refactors and a mysterious failing test you don't remember creating.\n\nFor Token, I forced myself into three very boring-sounding but incredibly useful modes:\n\n- **Build** – add new behavior\n- **Improve** – reshape internals without changing observable behavior\n- **Sweep** – fix a cluster of related bugs in one pass\n\nBefore each session, I'd literally type to the agent: *\"This is a **Build** session for split view Phase 3\"* or *\"This is an **Improve** session to extract modules from `main.rs`—no behavior changes allowed.\"* That one line changed everything.\n\nWhy? Because the agent's job is to optimize for whatever you point it at. If you don't declare the game you're playing, it will happily mix them:\n\n- \"While we're here, should we also clean up this enum?\"\n- \"This bug would be easier to fix if we rewrote the whole module.\"\n- \"I fixed the test by changing the feature.\"\n\nDeclaring a mode upfront gives you a contract:\n\n- **Build**: You're allowed to break things *temporarily* as long as we end up with a new capability.\n- **Improve**: You are **not** allowed to change the user-facing behavior; you can only move code around, rename things, and clarify responsibilities.\n- **Sweep**: You are allowed to change behavior, but only in service of a very narrow bug cluster.\n\nConcretely, here's how that played out in Token.\n\n### Build – Split view phases\n\nThe split view feature (multiple editors sharing documents) was a classic Build. The prompt looked like:\n\n> \"Implement split view Phase 3: AppModel wiring and layout tree, using EDITOR_UI_REFERENCE.md as the source of truth for viewport geometry.\"\n\nThe agent wasn't allowed to \"clean up\" unrelated modules. It was laser-focused on adding the `EditorArea` layout tree, wiring focus, and respecting the geometry rules in the reference doc.\n\n### Improve – Module extraction sprint\n\nLater, I had a 3,100-line `main.rs`. That became an **Improve** session:\n\n> \"Extract `app.rs`, `input.rs`, `view.rs`, `perf.rs` from `main.rs` without changing behavior. Tests must keep passing.\"\n\nThe goal was purely structural. At the end, `main.rs` was ~20 lines; the rest of the logic lived in well-named modules. Because the mode was Improve, we could be aggressive about moving code around—but we always had to route back to \"same behavior, more understandable shape.\"\n\n### Sweep – Multi-cursor selection bugs\n\nAfter multi-cursor shipped, I had a bucket of weird selection bugs: overlapping ranges, frozen secondary cursors, inconsistent collapse behavior. Instead of whack-a-mole, I declared:\n\n> \"This is a **Sweep** session: fix all known multi-cursor selection issues. Use MULTI_CURSOR_SELECTION_GAPS.md as the checklist.\"\n\nThe agent then worked through that gap doc: one pass, one area of the system, many small fixes. No new features, no architecture experiments.\n\nThe pattern generalizes: whatever you're building, teach your AI the difference between **Build**, **Improve**, and **Sweep**, and say which one you're doing before the conversation starts. It's the cheapest form of scope control you'll ever add.\n"
}
```

{
  "path": "~/code/website/src/pages/articles/ai-assisted-development-real-projects/sections/04-design-before-code.md",
  "content": "## Design Before Code\n\nEveryone says \"write the docs first.\" Almost nobody actually does, because writing docs is slow and not nearly as fun as watching the compiler scream at you.\n\nWith AI in the loop, those docs stop being optional. They become the **API** you expose to the agent.\n\nFor Token, I ended up with three kinds of design documents that paid off over and over:\n\n1. **Reference docs** – shared truth for tricky domains\n2. **Feature specs** – concrete plans for complex features\n3. **Gap documents** – \"this is 70% done and gross; here's what's left\"\n\n### Reference Docs: The Geometry Bible\n\nThe text editor UI is a geometry problem disguised as a productivity app. You're constantly converting between:\n\n- Buffer offsets\n- Line/column positions\n- Pixel coordinates\n- Viewport rectangles and scroll offsets\n\nIf you try to \"just code it,\" you get a mess of off-by-one scroll behavior and impossible-to-reason-about helper functions.\n\nSo I wrote `EDITOR_UI_REFERENCE.md`: a brutally literal description of how Token's viewport math works. It defines:\n\n- What a \"viewport\" is and how it relates to the underlying buffer\n- How scrolling behaves for keyboard, mouse wheel, and page navigation\n- How we clamp cursors, selections, and scroll positions at document edges\n- How split view and overlays share that same geometry\n\nThen I did something slightly masochistic but incredibly useful: I had the Oracle agent review the doc **before** we wrote much rendering code.\n\nThat review caught more than 15 issues that would have been bugs in code:\n\n- Inconsistent definitions of \"line height\" leading to fractional scroll positions\n- A division-by-zero edge case when the viewport height was smaller than one line\n- Off-by-one rules for \"last visible line\" vs \"line just offscreen\"\n- Cases where the scroll math would have allowed the cursor to drift slightly offscreen\n\nFixing those in a markdown file took minutes. Fixing them in code would have required re-threading those assumptions across `view.rs`, `editor_area.rs`, hit testing, and tests.\n\nThe hidden benefit: once you have a geometry bible, every future feature (\"center cursor on screen,\" \"overlay search results\") can say \"follow the behavior in `EDITOR_UI_REFERENCE.md`\" instead of re-litigating what \"scroll to reveal\" means.\n\n### Feature Specs: Multi-Cursor as a Written Contract\n\nMulti-cursor editing is one of those features where \"just implement it\" is a trap. There are too many edge cases, and the behavior has to feel **obvious**, not merely \"consistent with your current code.\"\n\nBefore writing serious code, I wrote `SELECTION_MULTICURSOR.md`. The structure was intentionally boring:\n\n- Existing data structures and where they live\n- New data structures we'd need\n- **Invariants** that must always hold\n- Keyboard shortcuts and which messages they fire\n- A multi-phase implementation plan\n- Success criteria and tests\n\nThe invariants section was the moral center of the doc:\n\n```rust\n// MUST maintain: cursors.len() == selections.len()\n// MUST maintain: cursors[i].to_position() == selections[i].head\n```\n\nThose two lines drove a lot of design: it forced us to treat cursors and selections as parallel arrays, updated in lockstep. When we later added deduplication and selection merging, the invariant acted like a unit test written in English.\n\nThe message section turned into a clean API surface:\n\n- `EditorMsg::MoveCursorWithSelection(Direction)`\n- `EditorMsg::ToggleCursorAtPosition { line, column }`\n- `EditorMsg::CollapseToSingleCursor`\n- …and so on.\n\nInstead of asking the agent, \"How do I support Cmd+Click to add cursors?\", I could say:\n\n> \"Implement Phase 4 from `SELECTION_MULTICURSOR.md`: multi-cursor basics. Use the messages and invariants already defined.\"\n\nThat conversation is dramatically easier to keep on track. The agent isn't inventing behavior on the fly; it's filling in code against a spec we already agreed on.\n\n### Gap Documents: From \"Feels Buggy\" to Checklists\n\nThe third document type only appears later in a feature's life: when something is 60–90% done, working *mostly* fine, but clearly not \"finished.\"\n\nFor multi-cursor, that was `MULTI_CURSOR_SELECTION_GAPS.md`. The pattern:\n\n1. **What's implemented**\n2. **What's missing or broken**\n3. **Design decisions for each gap**\n4. **Tests / success criteria**\n\nFor example, instead of \"selection merging seems off,\" the gap doc would say:\n\n- **Implemented:**\n  - `merge_overlapping_selections()` merges overlapping or touching ranges.\n  - Parallel vectors `cursors` and `selections` are updated in lockstep.\n- **Missing:**\n  - Tests for adjacent selections like `[0,5)` and `[5,10)`.\n  - Behavior when three or more selections collapse to the same position.\n- **Design notes:**\n  - Adjacent selections should merge into a single range.\n  - When merged, keep the lowest index as the \"primary\" cursor.\n- **Tests:**\n  - Given cursors at positions A, B, C with overlapping selections, assert we end up with one cursor/selection pair satisfying the invariants.\n\nGap docs are where the \"AI loves checklists\" thing really shows. Models struggle with \"this feels buggy.\" They excel at:\n\n> \"Here is a list of 9 concrete gaps. For each, either implement the missing behavior or add an explicit TODO explaining why we're deferring it. Keep the invariants intact.\"\n\nBecause the doc already encodes context, each Sweep session becomes:\n\n> \"Work through `MULTI_CURSOR_SELECTION_GAPS.md` top to bottom. Do not add new shortcuts or data structures.\"\n\nThat's the pattern I've come back to repeatedly:\n\n- Reference docs for **shared physics of the system**\n- Feature specs for **big changes**\n- Gap docs for **polish and bug sweeps**\n\nNone of this is glamorous, but it turns vague frustration into structured work the agent can actually help with.\n"
}
```

{
  "path": "~/code/website/src/pages/articles/ai-assisted-development-real-projects/sections/05-agent-config.md",
  "content": "## Agent Configuration\n\nThe last piece of the framework is the least exciting and the most leveraged: **tell the agent how your project works. Explicitly. In one place.**\n\nFor Token, that's `AGENTS.md` (and a mirrored `CLAUDE.md`):\n\n- How to build and run the project\n  - `make dev`, `make test`, `make test-one TEST=...`\n- Where the important modules live\n  - \"Editor state is in `src/model/`, rendering in `view.rs`, perf overlays in `perf.rs`\"\n- Naming conventions\n  - How we name messages, models, and modules\n- Any \"gotchas\"\n  - \"Don't call `cargo test` directly; always use `make test` so the right feature flags and environment variables are set.\"\n\nBefore this file existed, the AI did what any well-meaning junior dev would do: it made stuff up.\n\nIt would propose commands like:\n\n- `cargo test --all-features --no-fail-fast`\n- `cargo run --bin token-editor`\n\nTechnically reasonable, completely wrong for this project.\n\nOnce `AGENTS.md` spelled out:\n\n```text\nTo run tests: use `make test` or `make test-one TEST=...`.\nTo run the app: use `make dev` during development.\nDo NOT invent cargo flags; rely on the Makefile.\n```\n\n…the hallucinated incantations basically stopped. Every \"run tests\" instruction in a prompt could now say, \"and verify with `make test`,\" and the agent followed suit.\n\nYou don't need an elaborate setup to copy this pattern. Even for a small project, a single page is enough:\n\n- **How to build and run**\n- **How to run tests**\n- **Where the core logic lives**\n- **Any non-obvious constraints** (\"this script must run from the repo root,\" \"this service requires Redis running locally,\" etc.)\n\nThen you reference that page in your prompts:\n\n> \"You're working in the `Improve` mode. Before proposing changes, skim `AGENTS.md` to understand how to run tests and where the editor state lives.\"\n\nIt's boring glue. But it's the difference between an AI that constantly trips over your build system and one that feels like a teammate who read the onboarding doc before touching anything.\n"
}
```

{
  "path": "~/code/website/src/pages/articles/ai-assisted-development-real-projects/sections/06-case-study-multicursor.md",
  "content": "## Case Study #1 – Multi-Cursor Migration\n\nThe single biggest change in Token was going from \"there is one cursor\" to \"there might be N cursors at arbitrary positions.\" That sounds like a cute feature until you realize \"single cursor\" was baked into almost every file: movement, selection, delete/insert, viewport math, tests, even helper names.\n\nA full rewrite would have been a great way to ship nothing for three weeks, so we treated it as a structured migration instead of a heroic refactor.\n\n### Start with invariants, not code\n\nBefore touching any code, we wrote down what *must* stay true:\n\n```rust\n// MUST maintain: cursors.len() == selections.len()\n// MUST maintain: cursors[i].to_position() == selections[i].head\n```\n\nThese invariants showed up everywhere: in the design doc, in comments, and eventually in tests. They became the \"contract\" we could repeatedly hand to AI agents: \"you can change internals, but if these conditions ever break, you've done something wrong.\"\n\n### Migration helpers: pretending we still had one cursor\n\nThe next trick was to *lie* to the rest of the codebase.\n\nInternally, `EditorState` grew a `Vec<Cursor>`, but we kept an old-looking accessor so existing handlers and tests didn't explode immediately:\n\n```rust\n// Migration helper - old code still works\nimpl AppModel {\n    pub fn cursor(&self) -> &Cursor { &self.editor.cursors[0] }\n}\n```\n\nAnywhere that wasn't ready to think \"multi-cursor\" could keep using `cursor()` and stay blissfully ignorant. This bought us the freedom to convert one subsystem at a time instead of \"convert everything or nothing compiles.\"\n\n### Phased implementation: from primitives to behavior\n\nWe then split the migration into numbered phases that an AI agent could execute independently:\n\n- **Phase 0 – Per-cursor primitives.**  \n  Add low-level helpers that operate on \"cursor at index i\" without worrying about \"all cursors\" yet:\n\n  ```rust\n  fn move_cursor_left_at(&mut self, doc: &Document, idx: usize) { ... }\n  fn move_all_cursors_left(&mut self, doc: &Document) {\n      for idx in 0..self.cursors.len() {\n          self.move_cursor_left_at(doc, idx);\n      }\n  }\n  ```\n\n- **Phase 1 – All-cursors wrappers.**  \n  Once the per-cursor operations existed, we layered \"for all cursors\" functions on top (`move_all_cursors_left`, `move_all_cursors_right`, etc.), always ending with a dedup step when cursors collided.\n\n- **Phase 2–3 – Wire up handlers.**  \n  Movement handlers in `update` (arrows, Home/End, word movement, PageUp/Down) were switched from `cursor_mut()` to the new `move_all_*` APIs. Selection variants got separate \"with_selection\" helpers so we didn't accidentally collapse selections.\n\n- **Phase 4 – Tests.**  \n  We added explicit multi-cursor tests (\"two cursors at positions X and Y both move left\", \"colliding cursors are deduped\", \"each cursor keeps its own desired_column\") while keeping all single-cursor tests green.\n\n- **Phase 5 – Bug sweep.**  \n  Once behavior was mostly right, we ran a dedicated Sweep session: grep for everything that still looked suspiciously single-cursor and either delete the helper (`cursor_mut`) or wrap it in a multi-cursor equivalent.\n\nThis \"0–5\" structure mapped perfectly to the Build/Improve/Sweep modes from earlier: Build the new APIs, Improve the architecture around them, then Sweep for edge cases.\n\n### What went wrong anyway\n\nDespite the planning, we still stepped on a few rakes.\n\n**1. Some handlers quietly stayed single-cursor.**  \nA few movement paths were still calling helpers like `first_non_whitespace_column()` that implicitly read from the primary cursor's line. Everything compiled, most tests passed, and yet: only the main cursor jumped to the right column while the others stayed put. The fix was to push those helpers down into the per-cursor primitives so every cursor computed its own target column.\n\n**2. \"Only primary cursor moved\" – again.**  \nEven after introducing `move_all_cursors_left`, some call sites still used the old `move_cursor_left` out of habit. The symptom was familiar: you'd add multiple cursors, hit an arrow key, and only index 0 moved. The Sweep mode here was basically: list all the places that can move the cursor, then systematically assert \"this uses an all-cursor API, not a single-cursor one.\"\n\n**3. Overlapping selections and the late arrival of `merge_overlapping_selections()`.**  \nThe really fun bug showed up in real usage: add cursors on adjacent words, run \"Select Word\" a few times, and suddenly you'd get overlapping or touching selections that broke rendering and copy/paste semantics.\n\nThe data structure invariants were still technically true—`cursors.len() == selections.len()`—but the *user expectations* invariant (\"overlapping selections should behave like one big selection\") was not. The fix was to add:\n\n```rust\npub fn merge_overlapping_selections(&mut self) {\n    // 1. Sort by start position\n    // 2. Merge consecutive overlapping/touching ranges\n    // 3. Update both cursors and selections arrays in lockstep\n}\n```\n\nThat function didn't exist in the original plan; it emerged from actually *using* multi-cursor selection for a while and then formalizing \"what feels right\" into another invariant.\n\nThe takeaway: the framework helped us avoid total chaos, but it didn't make us omniscient. We still missed cases—what changed is that we had a clear place to record them (gap docs) and a repeatable way to sweep them up with the AI's help.\n"
}
```

{
  "path": "~/code/website/src/pages/articles/ai-assisted-development-real-projects/sections/07-case-study-splitview.md",
  "content": "## Case Study #2 – Split View Architecture\n\nSplit view was the other \"touch everything\" feature: multiple panes, tabs, and shared documents, all without turning the codebase into a tangle of special cases.\n\nThe naïve approach would have been to thread `Option<SecondaryEditor>` through half the code and pray. Instead, we treated it as an architectural upgrade with aggressive use of migration helpers so old code could keep thinking \"there is one document, one editor\" while the new world came online behind the scenes.\n\n### The core architecture\n\nWe made three key decisions:\n\n1. **Documents are shared, editors are not.**  \n   Documents live in a `HashMap<DocumentId, Document>`. Editors live in a separate `HashMap<EditorId, EditorState>`. Any number of editors can point at the same document, each with its own cursors, selections, and viewport. This matches how real editors behave: split the view, but don't duplicate the file.\n\n2. **Layout is a tree, not a grid of booleans.**  \n   We introduced a `LayoutNode` tree to represent editor groups and splits. A node is either a `Group` (a tab strip with one active editor) or a `Split` that contains children with a split direction and size ratios. That gives you arbitrary nesting: two vertical splits on the left, one big editor on the right, etc.\n\n3. **`EditorArea` owns the mess.**  \n   Instead of sprinkling layout logic through input, rendering, and state, we concentrated it in an `EditorArea` type that knows:\n   - which editor is focused,\n   - which document each editor shows,\n   - how the layout tree maps to actual rectangles on screen.\n\n### Migration helper: `single_document()` as a compatibility layer\n\nThe problem, of course, is that the rest of the codebase still assumed there was a single `Document` and a single `EditorState`. We didn't fix that overnight.\n\nInstead, we created migration helpers that *pretended* the new architecture was still the old one. Conceptually:\n\n```rust\nimpl AppModel {\n    pub fn document(&self) -> &Document {\n        self.editor_area.focused_document()  // Hides complexity\n    }\n}\n```\n\nEverywhere that previously grabbed `model.document` or `model.editor` could keep doing so. Internally, those accessors simply delegated to \"whatever editor is currently focused in the layout tree.\"\n\nThis gave us two huge advantages:\n\n- We could land the `HashMap<DocumentId, Document>` / `HashMap<EditorId, EditorState>` / `LayoutNode` refactor *first*, behind stable accessors.\n- Then we could gradually introduce split-specific behavior (open in new group, close split, cycle focus) without breaking the rest of the app every time.\n\n### AI in phases, not as an all-knowing architect\n\nSplit view was implemented almost entirely through AI sessions, but never as \"please build split view.\" Instead, we split it into narrow phases:\n\n- Phase 1: Define data structures (`DocumentId`, `EditorId`, `LayoutNode`, `SplitContainer`).\n- Phase 2: Implement layout traversal and basic hit-testing.\n- Phase 3: Wire rendering to the layout tree (each group renders its active editor).\n- Phase 4: Add commands to create/destroy splits and move focus.\n- Phase 5: Sweep for all the places that assumed `AppModel` had a single document/editor.\n\nEach phase came with a small spec (\"what's the shape of the tree,\" \"what does 'focused group' mean,\" etc.), and the migration helpers acted as guardrails. If something broke, it broke *inside* `EditorArea`, not everywhere.\n\nThe end result looks like a clean, intentional architecture. In reality, it was a chain of small, reversible steps, plus a lot of lying to the old code until it was ready to face the truth.\n"
}
```

{
  "path": "~/code/website/src/pages/articles/ai-assisted-development-real-projects/sections/08-case-study-cmdz.md",
  "content": "## Case Study #3 – The Cmd+Z Disaster\n\nNot every story is a grand architecture migration. Sometimes you just press Cmd+Z and your editor confidently types the letter `z` into the document.\n\nThat actually happened.\n\nOn macOS, the Command key is reported as a different modifier than Control. Token's undo handler, however, was enthusiastically Windows-pilled: it only checked `control_key()`. So on macOS, \"Cmd+Z\" looked exactly like \"plain Z\" and fell through to the text input path.\n\nHere's the embarrassing version:\n\n```rust\n// Before (broken on macOS)\nif modifiers.control_key() && key == \"z\" { ... }\n```\n\nIf you're on Linux or Windows, you never notice. On macOS, your first undo is a literal `z` character. Not ideal.\n\n### From single bug to Sweep mode\n\nWe could have fixed just that line and called it a day. Instead, we asked the more uncomfortable question: \"If undo is wrong, what else is wrong?\"\n\nThe answer, predictably, was \"quite a bit.\" Several other shortcuts were also only checking `control_key()` and ignoring `super_key()`. Some were subtly inconsistent across platforms. Rather than whack moles one at a time, we flipped into Sweep mode:\n\n- **Define the rule:** For cross-platform shortcuts, treat \"Ctrl on Windows/Linux\" and \"Cmd on macOS\" as equivalent. That means always checking both `control_key()` and `super_key()` for editor commands.\n- **Search the codebase:** Grep for every place we looked at modifiers + keys.\n- **Normalize behavior:** Update each shortcut to follow the same pattern, add comments where necessary, and make tests (or at least manual test notes) reflect the intended cross-platform mapping.\n\nThe final form of the undo check became:\n\n```rust\n// After (cross-platform)\nif (modifiers.control_key() || modifiers.super_key()) && key == \"z\" { ... }\n```\n\nNot exactly rocket science—but only once you've remembered that `super_key()` exists.\n\n### Why this matters for working with AI\n\nThis was a tiny change in terms of code, but a good example of how to use AI productively:\n\n- In **Build** mode, we had originally implemented undo/redo quickly, focusing on getting behavior right on one platform.\n- Once the bug surfaced, we switched to **Sweep** mode: \"audit all keyboard shortcuts for cross-platform correctness,\" not just \"fix Cmd+Z.\"\n- The AI could then take the simple rule (\"Ctrl or Cmd should both trigger these commands\") and apply it everywhere, instead of us manually eyeballing 20 handlers and inevitably missing one.\n\nIt also reinforced a more humbling lesson: no amount of upfront design will stop you from occasionally shipping \"Cmd+Z inserts z\" into your own editor. But if you've set up clear modes of work and you're willing to turn an embarrassing bug into a structured sweep, you at least get a better system out of it—and a good story for a blog post.\n"
}
```

  "content": "## What AI Was Good At (And Where It Faceplanted)\n\nBy the end of this project, the pattern was pretty clear: AI is fantastic at certain shapes of work, and absolutely terrible at others.\n\nOn the plus side, it crushed anything that looked like \"apply well-specified rules at scale.\"\n\n- **Turning docs into code.**  \n  Once I sat down and wrote `EDITOR_UI_REFERENCE.md`—with concrete cursor/scroll rules, coordinate systems, and examples—the assistant suddenly became a machine for producing consistent view/scroll math.  \n  I'd say: \"Update all the scrolling functions to use the reference doc's coordinate conventions\" and it would methodically fix each one, stay consistent across files, and even point out spots where my older code violated my own rules. Before that doc existed, similar prompts gave me a new, slightly different convention every time.\n\n- **Phased refactors.**  \n  Extracting `main.rs` into modules should have been stressful. Instead, I broke it into phases across seven threads: first isolate config loading, then input handling, then rendering, etc. Each session I'd say something like: \"We're in Improve mode; goal is to extract input handling into `input.rs`, no behavioral changes, tests must pass.\"  \n  The AI happily did the grunt work: moving functions, fixing imports, cleaning signatures. I mostly reviewed diffs and nudged naming. We never lost test coverage, and each phase stayed bite-sized.\n\n- **Systematic sweeps.**  \n  When you tell it \"find every handler that touches cursors and normalize how we update selections,\" it shines. Same with \"update every shortcut that uses modifiers to support Mac and Linux keymaps.\"  \n  This is exactly the kind of repetitive, easy-to-miss work humans get sloppy with at 11 p.m. and AI just…doesn't get tired.\n\n- **Research → synthesis.**  \n  The Librarian + Oracle pattern was a big win. I'd have the \"Librarian\" agent go read Zed's GPUI docs, GitHub issues, and blog posts, then ask the higher-level \"Oracle\" agent: \"Given this research, what's a sane strategy for mapping my keymap and modifier behavior?\"  \n  Instead of me skimming 10 tabs, it gave me a compact summary and trade-offs. I still made the final call, but it lined up the options and explained them in my language.\n\nWhere it **faceplanted** was wherever I tried to outsource judgment, taste, or long-term memory.\n\n- **Inventing architecture from nothing.**  \n  Early on, I asked some version of: \"What should the editor model look like? Design the whole thing.\"  \n  The answers looked impressive—nice boxes and arrows, plausible module names—but they were full of hand-wavy bits: \"we'll have a `SelectionManager` that efficiently handles all selection cases\" without acknowledging the gnarly invariants or performance constraints I cared about.  \n  The moment I pushed on details (\"how exactly do we keep multi-cursor selections consistent during reflow?\"), the design crumbled or flipped to a different abstraction. It was a great brainstorming partner, but a terrible chief architect.\n\n- **Remembering subtle invariants across days.**  \n  Anything that lived only in my head would get violated 2–3 sessions later. For example, I had an unwritten rule that \"a selection's end should never be before its start; we normalize on every update.\"  \n  A few days after establishing that rule in passing, we did a multi-cursor delete refactor. The AI \"simplified\" some code paths and suddenly we had inverted selections showing up in edge cases. That was on me: I'd never written the invariant down. Once it lived in `EDITOR_UI_REFERENCE.md`, the AI started defending it instead of breaking it.\n\n- **Vague tasks.**  \n  The worst prompts were things like: \"Make selection feel better.\" What came back was a mix of random tweaks: changed thresholds here, adjusted scroll speed there, touched three different modules. It was unreviewable and untestable.  \n  The same problem, framed as: \"Fix `DeleteBackward` for multi-cursor newline adjustment; current bug is X, expected behavior is Y\" turned into a clean, surgical patch with a test.\n\n### Failure anecdote #1\n\nI once asked it to \"rethink the whole selection architecture for performance and clarity.\" It dutifully generated a multi-layered design with managers and services, then started a huge refactor touching dozens of files. Halfway through, tests exploded and the new abstractions didn't actually solve the bottleneck. I ended up discarding the branch and instead wrote a tiny \"Selection invariants + hot paths\" doc and asked it to optimize just those hot paths. That worked.\n\n### Failure anecdote #2\n\nAnother time, after a good day working on cursor behavior, I came back two days later and asked, \"Extend the previous selection logic to support block selections.\" I assumed the AI \"remembered\" the subtleties we'd just ironed out. It didn't. It reintroduced an off-by-one bug we'd already fixed. The fix was not new code—it was me copying the invariant from the last session into the reference doc and telling it: \"These rules are non-negotiable; apply them here too.\"\n"
}

{
  "path": "~/code/website/src/pages/articles/ai-assisted-development-real-projects/sections/10-framework-recap.md",
  "content": "## Framework Recap – Apply This to Your Project\n\nIf you want to steal this approach wholesale, here's the distilled version. You don't need an editor project; this works for any non-trivial codebase.\n\n1. **Pick a non-trivial, bounded project.**  \n   Don't start with \"rebuild our entire monolith\" or \"redesign our infra.\" Pick something that's big enough to be real but small enough to finish: a new feature flag system, a refactor of your auth flows, a draft of a new syncing layer.\n\n2. **Write a 1–2 page reference doc for your core domain.**  \n   This is the keystone. Define your vocabulary, invariants, coordinate systems, failure modes. For an editor it was cursor semantics and scrolling rules. For you it might be \"how sessions, tokens, and users relate\" or \"what 'order status' actually means at each step.\"  \n   Don't aim for completeness—aim for \"good enough that a new teammate (or AI) would make the same choice you would on 80% of questions.\"\n\n3. **Define mode at each session start.**  \n   At the top of the thread, declare:\n   - Mode: **Build** (new capability), **Improve** (refactor/optimize), or **Sweep** (systematic, repetitive changes).\n   - Goal: one clear outcome.\n   - Non-goals: a few things you explicitly won't touch.  \n   This single habit reduced scope creep more than anything else. It also made it easier to say \"not now\" when the AI suggested interesting but off-track changes.\n\n4. **Create a simple `AGENTS.md`.**  \n   One small doc that answers:\n   - How to run tests\n   - How to run the app\n   - Where the key docs live\n   - Any \"house rules\" (coding style, commit conventions, etc.)  \n   Think of it as the README for your AI pair. Every new thread starts with: \"Context is in `AGENTS.md`; we're in Improve mode, see goal below.\"\n\n5. **For big features: write a feature spec + phased plan.**  \n   Before touching code, outline:\n   - What success looks like (user-visible behavior, constraints)\n   - A 2–4 phase plan (e.g., data model, API, UI, sweep)  \n   Then execute one phase per thread. \"Phase 1 only; no UI tweaks yet.\" The AI will happily try to ship everything in one go unless you gate it like this.\n\n6. **When you're \"almost done but something's weird,\" write a gap doc.**  \n   Instead of asking, \"Why does this feel janky?\" pause and jot a short \"Gap Notes\" doc:\n   - What works\n   - What feels off, with concrete examples\n   - Any hypotheses  \n   Then give *that* to the AI: \"We're in Improve mode; address these gaps, don't restructure anything else.\" This keeps you from random, unprincipled tweaking.\n\n7. **Schedule bug sweeps instead of random drive-bys.**  \n   Rather than fixing bugs wherever you spot them, collect them. Then run a dedicated \"Sweep\" session: \"Fix all cursor blinking edge cases from BUGS.md; no refactors.\"  \n   The AI is great at \"find every instance of X and patch it.\" Use that. It's terrible at \"oh, while we're here let's secretly refactor three modules.\"\n\nThat's it. If you do nothing else:\n\n- Write one good reference doc.\n- Declare a mode and goal at the start of each session.\n- Keep work in small, phased chunks.\n\nEverything else is just scaffolding to support those three habits.\n"
}

{
  "path": "~/code/website/src/pages/articles/ai-assisted-development-real-projects/sections/11-anti-patterns.md",
  "content": "## Failure Modes and Anti-Patterns\n\nI also learned a few ways to use AI that absolutely do not work. Ask me how I know.\n\n- **Chatbot-driven development.**  \n  This is when you open a chat and say, \"Just build the feature\" with no docs, no tests, and a fuzzy description. You might get code that compiles, but under the surface it's inconsistent with the rest of your system, ignores edge cases, and silently invents behaviors you'll be stuck with.  \n  If you see the AI guessing at your domain instead of quoting your docs back to you, that's a smell.\n\n- **Treating each session as a fresh start.**  \n  Early on, every day was a new chat with a different mini-spec. No shared `AGENTS.md`, no stable reference docs. The result was a pile of slightly incompatible decisions. I'd fix something Tuesday and unknowingly undo it Friday.  \n  Once I forced everything through the same small set of living docs, the AI stopped contradicting itself.\n\n- **Letting AI reorganize the codebase \"for cleanliness.\"**  \n  One day I let it go wild: big refactor, new modules, \"clearer\" layering, all in one Improve-mode session that wasn't actually scoped. It renamed files, moved types, adjusted imports, and touched dozens of call sites.  \n  Halfway through reviewing the diff, I realized I didn't actually know what problem we were solving. Tests were green, but I'd lost my mental map. That was the moment I stopped the experiment, created a `ROADMAP.md` and a `CHANGELOG.md`, and wrote down:\n  - What we're changing this week\n  - Why we're changing it\n  - What landed each day  \n  From then on, no large reorgs without a crisp Improve-mode goal tied directly to the roadmap.\n\n- **Mixing Build, Improve, and Sweep in one thread.**  \n  \"Let's add a new selection mode, clean up the old code, and also fix some related bugs\" sounds efficient. In practice, it's a mess. You get changes that are impossible to review because every file has new behavior, refactors, and bugfixes all tangled together.  \n  When I forced myself to pick one mode per thread, the quality of AI-produced diffs jumped immediately.\n\nThe pattern behind all of these: **AI amplifies whatever process you give it.** If your process is vague, impulsive, and undocumented, you'll get that back at scale. If you constrain it with clear docs, modes, and small phases, it becomes an extremely fast, very patient teammate instead of a chaos engine.\n"
}
```
