```
1) TL;DR
Token is already very close to having a “text physics engine”: `model/editor.rs` + the semantics encoded in `EDITOR_UI_REFERENCE.md` and the scrolling/cursor tests effectively *are* that layer, but they’re welded into the app model and the `Document`. With a bit of conceptual cleanup, you can imagine a standalone “text physics” core: a pure, buffer-agnostic module that owns cursors, selections, visual lines, and scroll state, talking to a generic text buffer and a separate renderer.

---

2) Recommended approach (simple path): what it would look like as a clean “text physics” layer

I’ll frame this as: (a) what you already have that approximates it, (b) what a standalone API could look like, (c) what’s inside vs outside.

### 2.1 What currently approximates “text physics” in Token

Roughly, the “physics” is spread across:

**Core state types in `model/editor.rs`**

- `Position`, `Cursor`, `Selection`
  - Semantics: line/column space, anchor/head, order (`start`, `end`, `is_reversed`, `contains`), “desired column” for vertical movement.
  - These are pure-ish and already look like reusable building blocks.

- `Viewport`
  - `top_line`, `left_column`, `visible_lines`, `visible_columns`.
  - This is a discrete, text-centric viewport (lines/columns), not pixel-based, which is exactly the kind of abstraction a physics engine would own.

- `ScrollRevealMode` + `ensure_cursor_visible` (snippet near lines 252–320)
  - Encodes the invariant: “when cursor leaves safe zone, adjust `viewport` in mode-specific ways (Minimal, TopAligned, BottomAligned, Centered).”
  - This is classic physics-layer behavior: translate cursor semantics into scroll changes.

- Multi-cursor movement functions (e.g., `move_cursor_left_at`, `move_cursor_right_at`, `move_cursor_up_at`, …)
  - These implement the semantics of cursor motion in line/column space, including cross-line moves and respecting line lengths.

- Selection helpers and rectangle selection (`RectangleSelectionState`)
  - Rectangular selection bounding boxes, mapping between positions and many cursors.

- Occurrence state / selection helpers (`OccurrenceState`, `word_under_cursor`, selection merging, etc.)
  - Higher-level, but still mostly “view of text + selection semantics”.

**Document interaction in `model/document.rs`**

- `Document` acts as a rope-powered buffer with:
  - `line_count`, `line_length`, `cursor_to_offset`, `offset_to_cursor`, `first_non_whitespace_column`, etc.
- The cursor movement and word/selection logic in `editor.rs` rely on these as their “world geometry” queries.

**Conceptual spec in `EDITOR_UI_REFERENCE.md`**

- Chapters 1, 4, 5, 6 especially are a *design document for a text physics layer*:
  - Coordinate systems (document vs viewport vs screen).
  - Line/column to pixel conversions, soft-wrap line layout, visible ranges.
  - Scroll margins, scroll modes, scroll trigger policies (`ensureCursorVisible`).
  - Mouse hit-testing and conversion `(x, y) -> (line, column)`.

**Tests in `tests/`**

- `cursor_movement.rs`, `scrolling.rs`, `selection.rs`, `multi_cursor.rs`, `document_cursor.rs`
  - Together, they already act as a conformance suite for “physics semantics”.

So, conceptually, Token *already* has a text physics core, but:

- It’s coupled to `Document` (ropey) and to Token-specific invariants.
- Logic is split between model (`model/editor.rs`) and update handlers (`update/editor.rs`).
- There’s no explicit “this is the physics API” boundary.

---

### 2.2 What a clean “text physics engine” API could look like

Very roughly, I’d imagine something in Rust like this, at the level of *interfaces*, not implementation:

#### Core traits (dependencies it needs from the outside)

```rust
/// Minimal read-only text interface the physics engine needs.
pub trait TextBuffer {
    fn line_count(&self) -> usize;
    fn line_length(&self, line: usize) -> usize; // columns (grapheme clusters / monospace cols)
    fn line_text(&self, line: usize) -> Option<&str>;

    // Optional helpers for word movement, etc:
    fn char_type_at(&self, line: usize, column: usize) -> Option<CharType>;
    // or: a higher-level “classification” API
}
```

For more advanced versions you might add:

- Grapheme cluster queries for RTL/Unicode correctness.
- Mappings between logical lines and folded/wrapped visual lines if wrapping/folding is inside the engine.

Separately, geometry input:

```rust
pub struct ViewMetrics {
    pub viewport_lines: usize,
    pub viewport_columns: usize,
    pub scroll_margins: ScrollMargins,
    pub wrapping: WrapMode, // Off | Soft { column: usize } | ByWidth { px_width: f32, char_width: f32 }
}
```

#### Physics state

A central state struct that is UI-agnostic:

```rust
pub struct TextViewState {
    pub cursors: Vec<Cursor>;
    pub selections: Vec<Selection>;
    pub active_cursor: usize;

    pub viewport: Viewport; // top_line, left_column, visible_lines, visible_columns

    pub scroll_mode: ScrollMode; // CURSOR_LOCKED, FREE_BROWSE, REVEAL_PENDING
    pub rectangle_selection: Option<RectangleSelectionState>;
    // maybe occurrence state, selection history (for expand/shrink) if you treat
    // those as part of selection semantics.
}
```

You basically already have most of this in `EditorState` and `RectangleSelectionState` and `OccurrenceState`; the main difference is that the engine’s state would be parameterized over `TextBuffer` and would not know about files, tabs, or commands.

#### Command API: “inputs” to the physics engine

Primary interface: given a current `TextViewState`, a `TextBuffer`, and a high-level motion/selection command, compute the next state and viewport.

```rust
pub enum Motion {
    CharLeft,
    CharRight,
    LineUp,
    LineDown,
    WordLeft,
    WordRight,
    LineStart,
    LineEnd,
    PageUp,
    PageDown,
    DocumentStart,
    DocumentEnd,
    // Possibly: LogicalLineUp/Down vs VisualLineUp/Down if wrapping is on
}

pub enum SelectionMode {
    Move,           // no selection (clear selections to cursors)
    Extend,         // shift+movement
    AddCursor,      // multi-cursor variant (e.g. alt+click / ctrl+alt+arrow)
}

pub enum ClickKind {
    Single,
    DoubleWord,
    TripleLine,
    Rectangular,
}

pub struct MouseEvent {
    pub x_px: f32,
    pub y_px: f32,
    pub modifiers: Modifiers,
    pub kind: ClickKind,
}
```

Engine entry points:

```rust
impl TextViewState {
    pub fn apply_motion<B: TextBuffer>(
        &mut self,
        buffer: &B,
        motion: Motion,
        selection_mode: SelectionMode,
        reveal_mode: ScrollRevealMode,
        metrics: &ViewMetrics,
    ) {
        // 1. Move cursors in line/column space (CharLeft, WordRight, etc.)
        // 2. Update selections according to SelectionMode
        // 3. Run scroll logic (ensure cursor visible) based on reveal_mode + metrics
    }

    pub fn handle_mouse_down<B: TextBuffer>(
        &mut self,
        buffer: &B,
        event: MouseEvent,
        metrics: &ViewMetrics,
    ) {
        // 1. Map (x_px, y_px) → (line, column) using metrics + viewport.
        // 2. Start / modify selection(s).
    }

    pub fn handle_mouse_drag<B: TextBuffer>(...) { /* update selections + rect-state */ }

    pub fn handle_scroll(&mut self, delta_lines: isize, delta_cols: isize, metrics: &ViewMetrics) {
        // free-browse scroll, clamped to buffer extents
    }

    pub fn recompute_viewport_lines<B: TextBuffer>(
        &self,
        buffer: &B,
        metrics: &ViewMetrics,
    ) -> VisibleLines {
        // computeVisibleLines / partial visibility etc.
    }
}
```

The key points:

- The physics engine accepts **normalized, editor-generic commands** (MoveLeft, WordRight, PageDown, click and drag), not raw keycodes or winit events.
- It returns **pure model changes**: positions, selections, scroll offsets, and visible ranges.
- It depends on an abstract `TextBuffer` and some geometry metrics; it doesn’t know about ropey, file paths, or rendering.

This is essentially what you already *do* in `update/editor.rs` + `model/editor.rs`, but asphalted together rather than through a narrow API.

---

### 2.3 Boundaries: what goes in vs. stays out

**In the “text physics” engine**

- **Coordinate spaces and transforms**
  - Document coordinates: `(line, column)`
  - Visual-line coordinates for soft wrap/folding, if you decide to include those.
  - Viewport coordinates: `{ top_line, left_column, visible_lines, visible_columns }`
  - Pixel/unit conversions *if* you want the engine to own click hit-testing and scroll margins expressed in lines/columns; the engine doesn’t have to know actual device pixels, only “char width/line height” if needed.

- **Cursor and selection semantics**
  - Single and multi-cursor move behaviors (char/word/line/document, home/end, page up/down).
  - Selection extension rules (shift+movement, select word/line, select all).
  - Rectangular selections and their expansion into per-line cursors.
  - Selection merging/deduping (e.g., when multi-cursors collapse on same position).

- **Scroll + cursor interaction**
  - Scroll modes (`CURSOR_LOCKED`, `FREE_BROWSE`, `REVEAL_PENDING`).
  - Scroll margins and “safe zone” logic.
  - “Ensure cursor visible” or “center cursor” behaviors for operations like goto line, search jump, etc.

- **Soft-wrap / visual line logic (if you choose)**
  - Mapping between logical lines and a sequence of visual lines.
  - Up/Down in wrapped mode (stay on same visual column, may change logical line).
  - Click hit-testing and visible-line computation in wrapped space.

- **Text classification for movement**
  - Word boundaries via `CharType` or similar for word-based motions.
  - Possibly sentence/paragraph semantics if you want fancier physics.

**Out of the engine (stays in Token-specific layers)**

- **Buffer implementation and storage**
  - Ropey `Rope`, undo/redo stacks (`EditOperation`), file I/O, autosave.
  - That all lives in `Document` or a “document model” layer.

- **Rendering and widgets**
  - Drawing glyphs, gutters, scrollbars, status bars, overlays, tab bars.
  - Any GPU or OS-specific rendering concerns.

- **Keymapping and input routing**
  - Mapping `winit` key/mouse events → semantic `Motion`/`SelectionMode`/ClickKind/etc.
  - Context-aware keybindings, command palette, etc. (as per `feature/KEYMAPPING.md`).

- **Language services and syntax highlighting**
  - Tokens, scopes, LSP integration, diagnostics.
  - These might query selections/cursors but don’t belong inside physics.

- **Application structure**
  - Tabs/splits (`EditorArea`), layout trees, active document selection.
  - Perf overlays, theming, workspace management, etc.

A useful litmus test:
> If an operation only needs `(TextViewState + TextBuffer + metrics)`, it belongs *in* the physics engine. If it needs file paths, themes, or global app routing, it stays *out*.

---

3) Rationale and trade-offs

**Rationale for viewing this as a distinct engine:**

- The combination of `model/editor.rs` + `EDITOR_UI_REFERENCE.md` is already a de facto *physics specification*:
  - There’s a well-defined grammar of motions and scrolling.
  - There are tests that encode expected behavior.
- Formalizing this as “text physics” clarifies:
  - Which invariants you expect to always hold (cursor/selection correspondence, safe-zone scrolling).
  - What other frontends (GUI/TUI/web) must implement to be “Token-like”.

**Trade-offs vs. keeping it as a monolithic editor model:**

- **Pros of separation**
  - Easier to reason about and test in isolation.
  - Could be reused across UIs (e.g. a TUI frontend, headless LSP-ish tools).
  - Cleaner mental model: document (storage), physics (interaction semantics), view (rendering), shell (app).
  - More obvious place to encode tricky rules like multi-cursor merging, expand/shrink-selection stacks, etc.

- **Cons**
  - Another abstraction layer; you’ll have to thread `TextBuffer` and metrics everywhere.
  - Some Token-specific shortcuts (e.g., directly calling `Document` methods from movement code) become indirect.
  - Harder to make quick, bespoke tweaks to cursor behavior if you feel constrained by a “generic” API.

Given that this is a thought experiment and not a refactor plan, the takeaway is: you already have the engine behaviors; the main change would be *drawing a clean API line* and allowing callers to treat it like a “black box” that updates cursor/selection/viewport given inputs.

---

4) Risks and guardrails

If you were to actually separate it:

- **Risk: over-generalizing too soon**
  - You might chase configurability (“support arbitrary bidi/wrapping/folding patterns”) and end up with a complex, leaky API.
  - Guardrail: start with the semantics Token already has (monospace LTR, simple wrap mode), design API for that; only generalize when you need to.

- **Risk: perf regressions**
  - Indirection via `TextBuffer` trait calls and wrap computations can introduce overhead.
  - Guardrail: keep interfaces coarse-grained (e.g., pass per-line text or precomputed classification arrays rather than char-by-char calls), and benchmark on large files.

- **Risk: mismatch between “physics crate” semantics and Token-specific UX**
  - You might discover that what feels right in Token is subtly Token-specific (e.g., exact merge rules for multi-cursor selection) and awkward to encode in a generic API.
  - Guardrail: prioritize “Token’s semantics first, reusability second”; design for *pluggable hooks* rather than over-abstracting.

- **Risk: scope creep into full text engine**
  - It’s easy to slide from “text physics” into “full text system” (layout, shaping, bidi, line breaking).
  - Guardrail: explicitly declare boundaries (as above) and keep layout/shaping in a separate layer.

---

5) When to consider the “advanced” separation path

Signals that might justify truly extracting this as a reusable engine:

1. **Multiple frontends**
   - You want a TUI version, a web version, or multiple editor-style products reusing Token’s movement/scrolling feel.

2. **Third-party adoption interest**
   - If people start asking “can I use Token’s movement + selection semantics in my app?” — that’s a sign the physics is a product in itself.

3. **Complex new features in the same layer**
   - Bidirectional text, multiple wraps/folds, richer selection modes (e.g., structural selections) that sit squarely in the physics domain and would benefit from being centralized.

4. **Difficulty evolving cursor/scroll behavior**
   - If changes to cursor/scroll semantics routinely break things because logic is scattered through `update/` and `view/`, then centralization as an engine can reduce blast radius.

If none of these are true and Token remains a single desktop app, the current semi-integrated architecture is perfectly reasonable and simpler to maintain.

---

6) Optional advanced path: what a fully separated “text physics engine” might resemble

If you *did* pursue this as its own crate (e.g. `token_text_physics`), a more advanced design could:

- **Have two nested layers:**
  1. **Core text dynamics**
     - Pure `(line, column)` space, multi-cursor + selection semantics.
     - Word/paragraph movement, expand/shrink selection, occurrence selection.
  2. **View/geometry
```

## Assistant

**Short answer:** You've effectively *done* most of this already, just without a formal API boundary.

**What Token already has:**
- `model/editor.rs` → The state (cursors, selections, viewport, scroll mode)
- `EDITOR_UI_REFERENCE.md` → The specification for the physics
- `update/editor.rs` → The operations (movement, selection)
- `tests/cursor_movement.rs`, `tests/selection.rs` → The conformance suite

**What a clean "text physics engine" would add:**
1. **A `TextBuffer` trait** that `Document` implements, so the physics doesn't know about ropey
2. **A narrow command API** (`Motion`, `SelectionMode`) instead of directly calling cursor methods
3. **An explicit boundary** where the engine only sees `(state + buffer + metrics)` → returns new state

**The API surface would be roughly:**
```rust
pub trait TextBuffer {
    fn line_count(&self) -> usize;
    fn line_length(&self, line: usize) -> usize;
    fn char_type_at(&self, line: usize, col: usize) -> CharType;
}

impl TextViewState {
    pub fn apply_motion<B: TextBuffer>(
        &mut self,
        buffer: &B,
        motion: Motion,        // CharLeft, WordRight, PageDown...
        selection: SelectionMode,  // Move, Extend, AddCursor
        reveal: ScrollRevealMode,
        metrics: &ViewMetrics,
    );
}
```

**Would it be worth separating?**
- **Yes if:** you want a TUI version, web version, or other editors reusing Token's "feel"
- **No if:** Token remains a single desktop app—the current semi-integrated approach is simpler

The interesting insight: your `EDITOR_UI_REFERENCE.md` is already the *specification* for this physics engine. You've done the hard intellectual work; the separation would just be drawing the API line.

char_type_at(&self, line: usize, column: usize) -> Option<CharType>;\n    \n    // For smart home\n    fn first_non_whitespace_column(&self, line: usize) -> usize;\n    \n    // Optional: for advanced features\n    fn line_text(&self, line: usize) -> Option<&str>;\n}\n```\n\nToken's `Document` would implement this trait.\n\n---\n\n## The Physics API\n\n```rust\nimpl TextViewState {\n    /// Apply a self,\n        buffer: &B,\n        position: Position,\n        metrics: &ViewMetrics,\n    );\n    \n    /// Free scroll (mouse wheel, scrollbar)\n    pub fn scroll(&mut self, delta_lines: isize, delta_columns: isize);\n    \n    /// Compute visible line range for rendering\n    pub fn visible_lines(&self) -> Range<usize>;\n}\n```\n\n---\n\n## Current Token Implementation\n\nToken already has one with lower index\n\n**15.5 Selection Merging**\n- Overlapping selections `[a, b)` and `[c, d)` where `c <= b` merge to `[a, max(b, d))`\n- Adjacent selections (touching but not overlapping) also   "diff": "````diff\nIndex: ~/code/rust-editor/docs/EDITOR_UI_REFERENCE.md\n===================================================================\n--- ~/code/rust-editor/docs/EDITOR_UI_REFERENCE.md\toriginal\n+++ ~/code/rust-editor/docs/EDITOR_UI_REFERENCE.md\tmodified\n@@ -3486,8 +3486,135 @@\n ```\n \n ---\n \n+## Chapter 15: System Invariants and Constraints\n+\n+This chapter documents the invariants that must always hold in a well-behaved text editor. These are the \"physics laws\" of the system—violating them leads to subtle bugs, rendering glitches, or undefined behavior.\n+\n+### 15.1 Cursor-Selection Invariants\n+\n+The most critical invariants relate to the parallel cursor/selection arrays:\n+\n+```\n+INVARIANT 1: cursors.len() == selections.len()\n+    Every cursor has exactly one corresponding selection.\n+    Violating this causes index-out-of-bounds during rendering or editing.\n+\n+INVARIANT 2: cursors[i].to_position() == selections[i].head\n+    The cursor position IS the selection head (the \"moving end\").\n+    The selection anchor is the \"fixed end\" from which selection extends.\n+    Violating this causes cursor to render in wrong position relative to highlight.\n+```\n+\n+These invariants must be maintained after every operation: movement, editing, undo/redo, mouse interaction.\n+\n+### 15.2 Selection Normalization\n+\n+Selections have direction (anchor may be before or after head), but must support normalized access:\n+\n+```\n+selection.start() → min(anchor, head)\n+selection.end()   → max(anchor, head)\n+selection.is_empty() → anchor == head\n+selection.is_reversed() → anchor > head\n+```\n+\n+**Rule:** After any text modification within a selection, the selection should be adjusted or collapsed. Inserting text at a selection replaces it; the cursor moves to the end of the inserted text.\n+\n+### 15.3 Viewport Safety Zone\n+\n+After any cursor movement operation, the primary cursor must be visible within the \"safe zone\":\n+\n+```\n+VERTICAL:\n+    cursor.line >= viewport.top_line + margins.top\n+    cursor.line <= viewport.top_line + visible_lines - margins.bottom - 1\n+\n+HORIZONTAL:\n+    cursor.column >= viewport.left_column + margins.left\n+    cursor.column <= viewport.left_column + visible_columns - margins.right - 1\n+```\n+\n+If the cursor violates these bounds, the viewport must scroll to restore the invariant. The scroll strategy (minimal, centered, aligned) may vary by operation type.\n+\n+### 15.4 Multi-Cursor Deduplication\n+\n+```\n+INVARIANT: No two cursors may occupy the same (line, column) position.\n+```\n+\n+After any operation that moves multiple cursors, deduplicate by:\n+1. Sort cursors by position\n+2. Remove duplicates, keeping the cursor with lower original index\n+3. Remove corresponding selections in lockstep\n+\n+Common collision scenarios:\n+- Two cursors on short line, both press End\n+- Cursors on adjacent lines, one moves down\n+- Word movement where both cursors land on same word boundary\n+\n+### 15.5 Selection Merging\n+\n+When selections overlap or touch, they should merge:\n+\n+```\n+Overlapping: [a, b) and [c, d) where c < b\n+    → merge to [min(a,c), max(b,d))\n+\n+Touching: [a, b) and [b, c)\n+    → merge to [a, c)\n+```\n+\n+**When to merge:**\n+- After SelectWord/SelectLine with multiple cursors\n+- After selection extension that causes overlap\n+- Before clipboard operations (copy/cut)\n+\n+**Merge algorithm:**\n+1. Collect (start, end, cursor_index) for all selections\n+2. Sort by start position\n+3. Sweep: if next.start <= current.end, extend current.end\n+4. Rebuild cursor/selection arrays from merged results\n+\n+### 15.6 Edit Position Adjustment\n+\n+After a text edit, cursor/selection positions may need adjustment:\n+\n+```\n+INSERT at position P with length L:\n+    All positions > P: shift by +L\n+    All positions == P: depends on insert semantics (usually move to end of insert)\n+\n+DELETE range [start, end):\n+    All positions in range: collapse to start\n+    All positions > end: shift by -(end - start)\n+    All positions < start: unchanged\n+```\n+\n+For multi-cursor edits, process in reverse document order to avoid offset invalidation.\n+\n+### 15.7 Undo/Redo Cursor Restoration\n+\n+```\n+INVARIANT: Undo restores cursor/selection state to before the operation.\n+INVARIANT: Redo restores cursor/selection state to after the operation.\n+```\n+\n+This requires storing cursor/selection snapshots with each edit operation, not just text changes.\n+\n+### 15.8 Viewport Bounds\n+\n+```\n+INVARIANT: viewport.top_line >= 0\n+INVARIANT: viewport.top_line <= max(0, line_count - 1)\n+INVARIANT: viewport.left_column >= 0\n+```\n+\n+The viewport should never scroll past the document boundaries, with the exception that the last line should be scrollable to the top of the viewport (common \"overscroll\" behavior).\n+\n+---\n+\n ## Appendix A: Event Flow Example\n \n User presses Down Arrow while cursor is at bottom margin:\n \n````",

  ]
}
